   1|                                                                                     In Memoríam
   2|                                                                  Jane Doyle was born in Fitchburg on April 17, 1941. She was a
   3|                                                                  graduate of the Mary A. Burnham School for Girls and a 1963
   4|                                                                  graduate of Skidmore College with a BSN in Nursing. Jane
   5|                                                                  earned an MS in Nursing from Boston University and an MBA
   6|                                                                  in Health Care Administration from Western New England
   7|                                                                  College in 1984. Jane was a longtime resident of Lunenburg.
   8|                                                                  Jane was a member of the National Teachers Association,
   9|                                                                  National Educational Association, Sigma Theta Tau-Nursing
  10|                          Honor Society and Health Alliance Guild, Lunenburg Woman's Club, Lunenburg
  11|                          Friendly Seniors and the Lunenburg Turkey Hill Family Lions Club. She was the
  12|                          Chairperson of the Lunenburg Community Council, member of the Lunenburg Council
  13|                          on Aging, Past-President of the Lunenburg-Townsend Rotary Club, and recipient of the
  14|                          Paul Harris Fellow Award, a Rotary International Award for Service. In 2007, Jane
  15|                          discovered that there was no local food bank in town and coordinated meetings with
  16|                         fellow Lions' Club members and Fire Chief Glenny to create a space for residents to drop
  17|                          off and pick up food. Her vision lives on today.
  18|                                                          Charles P. Deming, Sr. was a lifelong resident of Lunenburg. Charlie
  19|                         graduated with the Lunenburg High Class of 1952. He worked as a firefighter for the
  20|                         Lunenburg Fire Department for 49 years and was an EMT for the first Lunenburg
  21|                          Rescue Squad. Mr. Deming was employed by General Electric Company for 35 years
  22|                          and was a member of the GE Credit Union's Board of Directors. He served as a
  23|                          Lunenburg Cemetery Commissioner. Charlie was treasurer of the 275th Anniversary
  24|                          Committee and the LHS Athletic Hall of Fame Committee. In 2012, he proudly received
  25|                          the SHS Alumnus of the Year Award. Charlie coached Lunenburg's Little League
  26|                          baseball teams, Biddy League basketball teams and was an EMT for Pop Warner
  27|                         football teams. He was actively involved in many sports and was a member of the GE
  28|                          Golf League and Lunenburg Men's Softball League. Charlie was known as a "human
  29|                          GPS"; he could give you directions to anywhere in Massachusetts and knew Lunenburg
  30|                          like the back of his hand; its highways, back roads, forests, fields and waterways. He
  31|                          was a talented woodworker and craftsman; family and friends treasure beautiful
  32|                          reminders of his creativity and generosity.
  33|                                                                                                3

===PAGE 7=== (ocr, 26 lines)
   1|                                                                                          LUNENBURG
   2|                          Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of
   3|                          approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
   4|                          approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
   5|                          City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east
   6|                          by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend
   7|                          Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters
   8|                          in the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town
   9|                          are generally administered by a five member Board of Selectmen and a Town Manager. Local
  10|                          school affairs are administered by an elected seven member school committee while local taxes
  11|                          are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all appointed on an at-large basis for
  12|                          staggered three-year terms.
  13|                          Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies
  14|                          account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development. Lake
  15|                          Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  16|                          remainder of Lunenburg. This district was developed as a housing base for many factory
  17|                          workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the
  18|                          characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  19|                          the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area of town, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  20|                          developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The third body of
  21|                          water is Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  22|                          that have now been modified to year-round residences. The remainder of Lunenburg which
  23|                          originally consisted largely of farms and orchards has slowly developed into single family
  24|                          housing. Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily
  25|                          along the two major arteries (Routes 2A and 13), and the town's peripheries, due largely to the
  26|                          close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems.

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                      REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                       The Town continued to make important progress in 2014 in many areas, including: School Building; Open Space
   3|                        Acquisition; Traffic Safety Improvements; Increased Public Transportation; and Organizational Improvements. In 2014, the
   4|                       School Building Committee and School Committee brought forth, to Town Meeting, a comprehensive plan for a new Middle
   5|                       School/ High School Building. This plan was the culmination of months' worth of review, study and public input. The plan
   6|                       and project was approved at Town Meeting and by an overwhelming 2 to 1 majority at the ballot. The building is well under
   7|                       construction and set to open in September 2016. Fifty-eight (58%) of the project's eligible costs will be paid through a grant
   8|                       from the MA School Building Authority.
   9|                       In 2014, both the Conservation Commission and the Open Space Committee prepared and adopted a set of review criteria for
  10|                       evaluating available land for public ownership. Each presented its set of criteria to the Board of Selectmen for acceptance.
  11|                       The criteria established proved helpful in evaluating two separate Chapter Land Right of First Refusal options during the year
  12|                       and also in supporting the Town's application for a Local Acquisitions for Natural Diversity (LAND) grant for the purchase
  13|                       of the William H. & Saundra B. Lane Property at Lunenburg Town Forest. This property, about 167 acres in total, provides
  14|                       critical access to the existing Town Forest at a very affordable price. Purchase of the property, which occurred in January
  15|                       2015, will serve to protect the headwaters of the Mulpus Brook for the long term.
  16|                       The Town began a comprehensive review of its Traffic Rules and Orders in the fall of 2014. As a result of this review, it was
  17|                       determined that a number of existing traffic signs had not been properly authorized, requiring corrective action. Additionally,
  18|                       the Board reviewed several different intersections which were called out as being particularly unsafe. After review, the
  19|                        Board determined to authorize stop signs at the following locations to improve pedestrian and vehicular safety.
  20|                             • Flat Hill Road and Sunset Lane
  21|                             • Highland Street; Main Street and Oak Avenue
  22|                             • Mulpus Road and Townsend Harbor Road
  23|                       The Town also continued to work on the redevelopment of Summer Street, a project slated to begin in 2016. In 2014, the
  24|                       Town committed $230,312 from Chapter 90 Funding to fund the Town's portion of the 75% design. The project is expected
  25|                       to cost about $7M, all of which will be funded though the Regional Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP), which is a grant
  26|                       of State & Federal funds.
  27|                       One of the most exciting projects is 2014 was the implementation of enhanced public transportation within Lunenburg. The
  28|                       Town pays both an annual assessment to the MART and to the MBTA. The assessment to MART pays for local/ regional
  29|                       transportation. The assessment to the MBTA pays for access to the commuter rail system in Fitchburg and Leominster. The
  30|                       Town learned through MART that there is a provision in State Law that would allow the Town to use its MBTA Assessment
  31|                       to purchase additional local transportation, at no additional cost to the taxpayer. Accordingly, the Town undertook a review
  32|                       of residents' transportation needs and began working with MART to provide additional local transportation. The additional
  33|                        services are slated to begin in 2015.
  34|                       One final noteworthy item is the creation of the Land Use Director position which was approved by the 2014 Annual Town
  35|                       Meeting. The Director is responsible for coordinating the activities of all of the Boards and Departments involved in
  36|                       regulating land use and land development in town. The Director will fill the existing role of Planning Director/ Town
  37|                       Planner, a new role of Economic Development Director, and also serve as a coordinator of the activities of all Boards and
  38|                        Departments within the Land Use group. Additionally, the Director will coordinate with both the Sewer Commission and
  39|                       Department of Public Works on utility, storm water management, drainage, road extension or development and/ or other
  40|                       related land development matters. The Town hired its first Land Use Director, Adam Burney, in September 2014.
  41|                       I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their time and many
  42|                       talents to make Lunenburg a better place. Thank you all for your dedicated service.
  43|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 47=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                                                                              BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   2|                          The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building at 960
   3|                          Massachusetts Avenue. The office hours for the Building Department are Monday and
   4|                          Wednesday 8:00 A.M. to 4:00 P.M. and Tuesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. to 6:00 P.M., closed
   5|                         on Friday. Michael J. Sauvageau                        serves as the Building Commissioner, John Morrealle is the
   6|                          Assistant Building Inspector and Lisa Normandin is the Administrative Assistant for the
   7|                         department.
   8|                          Electrical and Plumbing and Gas permits are now processed by the Building Department.
   9|                          Building permits for 2014 reflected a 26% increase from the prior year. There were several new
  10|                         single family homes constructed and while renovations, additions, roofing, decks and siding
  11|                         permits increased from the past year, the greatest increase has been in rooftop solar panel
  12|                         installation permits. The Emerald Place condominium project continues to move toward the
  13|                         project completion. The new junior/high school project was permitted in the fall and as of this
  14|                         report the foundation is in and the steel erection of the building is underway.
  15|                         The 2009 International Building Code and the 2009 International Residential Code are the
  16|                         current building codes of the Commonwealth. In addition, as of July 1, 2014, Lunenburg became
  17|                         a "Stretch Code" community. Homeowners and contractors are encouraged to review the
  18|                         requirements of this code prior to permitting.
  19|                          Carbon monoxide detectors are required by law to be installed in every home regardless of the
  20|                         age of the dwelling. Also, all swimming pools are required to obtain a building permit and an
  21|                          electrical permit. Inflatable pools that do not have code required fencing around them do not
  22|                         meet the building code and should be removed and permitted prior to installation.
  23|                          Residents should be aware that inflatable swimming pools do not meet code compliance
  24|                         requirements. Please contact our office to determine what step need to take place for safety.
  25|                          The Commonwealth of Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and
  26|                          corporations who bid or perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alteration, additions,
  27|                          etc.) for work exceeding $1000.00 be registered with the Commonwealth. This office will
  28|                          enforce this provision; however you should ask you contractor about his/her registration. Please
  29|                          contact this office with any questions about this requirement or call any information regarding
  30|                         zoning regulations or building code issues.
  31|                                                                                               47

   1|                                                                                 COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                          The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   3|                         supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   4|                          General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include; "setting policy for the administration of elder programs
   5|                         or services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues."
   6|                          The Council on Aging staff consists of; Doreen C. Noble, Director; Susan Doherty, Administrative
   7|                          Assistant & Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach; JoAnne Brazell & Ruth Swiecicki, Food
   8|                         Service Coordinators; Elsa Watson & Judy Alario, Diet Assistants, Gerry Beloin, and James McGuigan.
   9|                         Board Members are; Cheryl Moisan, Jacquelyn Dwyer, Kevin McNally, Pete Lincoln, Sarah Grant, Betty
  10|                          DiGiacomo, Diane Nowd, Brain Guenard, Deb, Seeley, Judith Tarbell and Deb Lincoln.
  11|                         In FY 14 the Council on Aging provided 10,453 units of service to732 participants. We assisted residents
  12|                         age sixty and over from the town itself with 62% of them women, and 38% of them men.
  13|                         The chart below shows some of the ongoing services that we provide:
  14|                         Transportation                                                               Tax Assistance, SHINE
  15|                         Congregate Lunch, Coffee Shop, Pancake Breakfast                               Lunch Bunch Club
  16|                         Traveling Tooth Fairy, Pet Therapy Days                                        Cribbage, Bridge, Bingo, Wii Bowling
  17|                         Chair Yoga, Walking club, Stretch & Tone, Line                                 Bocce, Mah Jong, Water Colors
  18|                          Dancing
  19|                          TRIAD, File for Life                                                          Meals on Wheels
  20|                         B/P, Glucose, and Flu Clinics                                                  Friendly Visitors
  21|                         Volunteer Recognition Program                                                  Manicure, Pedicures, Reflexology,
  22|                          Book Club, Movies, Puzzle Swap                                                Educational, legal, financial & health related
  23|                                                                                                        workshops
  24|                         Our funding comes from different sources. In FY 14 we received a total of $121,695 from the municipal
  25|                         budget for salaries and expenses. The Montachusett Area Regional Transit Authority (MART) funds the
  26|                         salaries of our van drivers and half time for our dispatcher. We also receive funding from the
  27|                         Massachusetts Department of Elder Affairs. The Supporters of the Eagle House, Inc. a non-profit 501(c)
  28|                         3 organization is the fundraising group for the senior center. Their contributions are invaluable and they
  29|                         are ever ready to help us with things that cannot be covered by the municipal budget. Anyone wishing to
  30|                         make a donation to aid us in our services to the elderly can make donations to: The Supporters of the
  31|                          Eagle House, Inc., Eagle House Senior Center, 25 Memorial Drive. A donation to the Eagle House is a
  32|                          nice way to honor the memory of a loved one.
  33|                         Staffing at the Eagle House consisted of a full-time director; one (35 hour a week) administrative
  34|                         assistant, one (24 hour a week) outreach worker and two food service coordinators who worked a total of
  35|                          15 hours a week along with two diet aides who work 8 hours a week. We also have 2 van drivers who
  36|                                                                                               49

===PAGE 50=== (ocr, 18 lines)
   1|             worked a combined total of 30-35 hours a week. Our staffing levels are minimal for a facility of our size,
   2|            our programing levels are extremely high and the amount of services we provide is astronomical,
   3|            therefore, volunteers are the backbone of the center, without them many of the services we provide would
   4|             not be in place.
   5|             Volunteer jobs range from meals drivers, to Friendly visitors, to kitchen assistants, and program
   6|            facilitators. In addition we have wonderful people who donate knitted items that are sent to schools,
   7|            hospitals, maternity wards and the veterans' homes. Many of the town's elderly not only volunteer at the
   8|             Eagle House but, also give of their time and talents to the town and other community organizations. This
   9|            year Eagle House volunteers clocked 3000 hours of service. We also had 11 people participate in the
  10|             Senior Tax Work Off program and they provided over 900 hours of labor to various town departments
  11|            and the Lunenburg Public School System.
  12|             Without the generosity and overwhelming support of the community many of the programs we are able
  13|            offer would not be possible.
  14|            Respectfully submitted,
  15|             Doreen C. Noble, Director

===PAGE 51=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                     DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|                                     The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park,
   3|                         Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one
   4|                         department organizing and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the residents of the
   5|                         community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope of services with a high degree of
   6|                         efficiency.
   7|                         DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   8|                                     "Public Works is either working or on call twenty four hours a day, three hundred and
   9|                         sixty five days a year to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related
  10|                         emergency that may arise."
  11|                                     We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town,
  12|                         and ensure others are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but
  13|                         rather to make sure efforts are not forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday
  14|                         service.
  15|                                     Major events over the past years have highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at
  16|                         the table and is a major player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire
  17|                         acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required services of others.
  18|                         We must continue to build on this awareness.
  19|                                     This department must also reach out to the public we serve and educate them to the fact
  20|                         that the common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated
  21|                         staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid
  22|                         waste collection and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management facilities and
  23|                         grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are the expected
  24|                         services supporting everyone in the community.
  25|                                     We will work to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather
  26|                         support for annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  27|                         PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  28|                                     The updated Pavement Management Plan, inclusive of an electronic database and a
  29|                         revised Town-wide pavement defect analysis, identifies the same backlog of work as the original
  30|                         document established in 2000. The backlog is now estimated at close to ten million dollars. A
  31|                         complete survey of every road is underway, but in general, since the level of funding has not
  32|                         significantly increased, pavement conditions continue to deteriorate more steadily than the repair
  33|                         and rehabilitation of Town ways.
  34|                                     Road projects typically extend over two construction seasons and overlap from one fiscal
  35|                          year to the next.
  36|                                     Improvements: milling and overlay on a portion of Goodrich St. from Reservoir Rd., top
  37|                         course on Northfield Rd from Gilchrest St. to the golf course including asphalt berms to control
  38|                         storm water, and an overlay on Fish St. to Leominster Rd. The scope of work included: a
  39|                         leveling course of asphalt installed one season and left over the winter, followed by a top or
  40|                         finish course the next season, sections of "berm" or curbing, shoulder work, minor drainage
  41|                         improvements and driveway aprons.
  42|                          Documentation provided through a "pot hole" log shows that one third of all the available work
  43|                         days during the season are dedicated to patching and other temporary repairs to similar
  44|                                                                                               51

===PAGE 52=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                        roadway failures. As defects are identified we respond to every location in a timely
   2|            manner. This has proven to be the least effective method of road repair. New pot holes appear
   3|            each spring particularly after a severe winter, and often staff revisits the same area year after
   4|            year.
   5|                        The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations however this
   6|            application is installed as a temporary solution with an effective life span of around five years.
   7|            Many streets that have been "sealed" are well beyond that time frame. This process involves the
   8|           installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to cracks or separations in the pavement.
   9|            The Pavement Management Plan also identifies specific areas where this method is the best
  10|            option. Please understand this method does not take the place of curb to curb paving, and is not
  11|            applicable in areas of other more serious defects.
  12|                        The revised Pavement Plan, no matter how complete, is of little value without an
  13|           identified source of funding. Lack of revenue continues to be the major detriment to improving
  14|            the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of funding is the Massachusetts
  15|            Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program. The estimated value of our 86 miles of
  16|            paved streets is by industry standards, calculated to be 62 million dollars. The General Highway
  17|            maintenance line within the operational budget has remained virtually unchanged for two
  18|            decades while the overall omnibus budget has nearly tripled to a value in excess of 30 million
  19|            dollars. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement program a substantial infusion of
  20|            funds is necessary. The conservative estimate remains at approximately 1.2 million dollars per
  21|            year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing allocations. Applying $400,000-$500,000 a
  22|            year to a backlog of work that approaches 10 million dollars places this department and the
  23|            Town as a whole in dismal failure. Many roads are compromised to the degree that only a major
  24|            reconstruction can bring them to an acceptable quality.
  25|            FLEET MAINTENANCE
  26|                        The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all town vehicles
  27|            and equipment excluding Fire Dept. apparatus. High performance police units, park, cemetery,
  28|            and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by
  29|            department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and
  30|           therefore more costly. Due to the volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and all
  31|            petroleum based products continues to be a moving target. Applying a solid budget number to
  32|            gasoline and diesel fuel costs is extremely difficult for Town Officials as well as department
  33|           heads.
  34|                        Throughout this year we installed a vehicle maintenance software program that has
  35|            proven to be a valuable tool. Each repair is logged by department and vehicle identification
  36|            number and memorialized in a data bank. Every vehicle can be tracked for part numbers,
  37|            required services, as well as frequency and cost of repairs over the life of the equipment.
  38|            FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  39|            The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  40|                              DPW Garage 520 Chase Road                                          Public Safety Complex
  41|                              Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                                          Town Hall
  42|                              Cemetery Bldg. Holman St.                                          Eagle House
  43|                              Town Beach                                                         Town Common

  45|                                                                                               52

===PAGE 53=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                          Old Primary School                                                 Veterans Park
   2|                                          All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
   3|                              The ever increasing workload made necessary the addition of a second individual to assist
   4|                       with repairs and maintenance. The increase in staff by only one has significantly lessened the
   5|                       need for outsourcing, specifically preventative maintenance. Staff remains on-call 24 hours a
   6|                       day, 365 days per year.
   7|                              Staff keeps buildings compliant and protects the safe running of the work environment. Any
   8|                       significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of disrupting or
   9|                       disabling necessary services provided to the community. The facilities support staff are problem
  10|                       solvers and technicians with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction
  11|                       trades. They deliver services or oversee those functions provided by specialized subcontractors.
  12|                       Staff manages and forecasts all of the building assets and prioritizes replacement costs. With the
  13|                       use of building maintenance software, requests for service are acknowledged immediately,
  14|                       prioritized, and completed in a timely manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted
  15|                       upon completion of the tasks a history of the repair is filed electronically.
  16|                       ENVIRONMENTAL
  17|                       Storm Water
  18|                                    The Environmental Protection Agency has not yet set the requirements for the next phase
  19|                       of storm water regulation and permits. As we await the potential of yet another unfunded Federal
  20|                       mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
  21|                                    The DPW, as a result of limited funding is forced to become reactionary to roadway
  22|                       drainage issues. A capital program in the 1990's earmarked $20,000.00 annually toward
  23|                       rehabilitation/installation of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding
  24|                       placed the financial burden upon the overall General Highway maintenance allocation effectively
  25|                       reducing drainage repairs.
  26|                                    As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are
  27|                       addressed. Little or no preventative maintenance, short of catch basin cleaning can be done due
  28|                       to a lack of available resources. At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall, drainage
  29|                       structures in close proximity to open water are cleaned. Other structures throughout the Town are
  30|                       addressed as time and resources allow.
  31|                       Waste Water
  32|                                    The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. In an effort to become
  33|                       more efficient the Sewer Commission has outsourced much of the day to day inspections and
  34|                       preventative maintenance of the overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations.
  35|                       Business management and oversight of the subcontractor remain the responsibility of the DPW.
  36|                       Tree Removal
  37|                       The infamous "Ice Storm of '08" continues to detrimentally affect trees in the Town right-of-
  38|                       way. Previously unnoticed damage together with dying limbs creates a backlog of tree felling
  39|                       and pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget. The department is unable to fulfill each resident
  40|                       request and must prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and
  41|                       property. A more efficient approach would involve a survey of all Town owned trees with a
  42|                       generated list of those requiring attention. Until a plan is in place, the DPW will act in a
  43|                                                                                               53

===PAGE 54=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                               reactionary mode. All work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling of small
   2|                   diameter trees is outsourced due to the required use of a bucket truck and a licensed professional.
   3|                   Each spring DPW staff moves Town-wide removing and disposing of woody debris within the
   4|                   right-of-way.
   5|                   Landfill
   6|                               The "capped" landfill off Young's Road is in compliance with the Division of
   7|                   Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as well as
   8|                   the possibility of groundwater contamination.
   9|                               The frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of
  10|                   consistently favorable results. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a
  11|                   regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  12|                                Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings,
  13|                   both determined by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials
  14|                   through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires
  15|                   yearly testing of these materials.
  16|                               On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage
  17|                   the annual composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to
  18|                   our residents and add a valuable component to our recycling program. There has to date been no
  19|                   dedicated funding toward the organization or long range remediation of this location. Once again
  20|                   dollars will be taken from the General Highway line to maintain this location while keeping it
  21|                   environmentally sate.
  22|                   Solid Waste
  23|                               Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the
  24|                   curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc.
  25|                   Lake Shirley Dam
  26|                               In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley
  27|                   Association turned over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total
  28|                   reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed
  29|                   solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship
  30|                   of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as
  31|                   well as an emergency action plan was determined to come under the umbrella of the Department
  32|                   of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance
  33|                   with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a
  34|                   professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection
  35|                   resulted in a positive report.
  36|                   WINTER OPERATIONS
  37|                               Snow and ice present a major challenge to our town. Each Town department and the
  38|                   safety of the general public depend on the ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe
  39|                   condition despite the severity or the duration of snow and ice events. To that end we develop a
  40|                   winter operations procedure based upon a well thought out plan that must be flexible due to the
  41|                   unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is expected and must be
  42|                   delivered to every part of town in a timely manner.
  43|                                                                                               54

===PAGE 55=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                                   Preparations begin with successful implementation of the Capital Plan. Rotation of the
   2|                       fleet in order to reduce breakdowns and minimize repairs is contingent upon resident support.
   3|                       Many of the front line vehicles are well beyond the optimum time for replacement. In the fall salt
   4|                       and sand are delivered to the Chase Road facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other
   5|                       communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for salt. Individually the Town also
   6|                       solicits bids for road sand. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and
   7|                       prepares the trucks, sanders and plows for winter operations. During the construction season road
   8|                       crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to
   9|                       eliminate obstacles to plowing. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations.
  10|                       Typically DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  11|                                    Generally it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the
  12|                       overall cost. For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less
  13|                       than a long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically,
  14|                       the department operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average
  15|                       winter.
  16|                       PARKS
  17|                                   In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners, DPW staff oversees and
  18|                       maintains all parks and grounds exclusive of school property. A full time Park employee was
  19|                       replaced with seasonal workers. Much of the Town wide landscape maintenance is outsourced,
  20|                       resulting in a significant savings. The multi-use track complex remains under the control of the
  21|                       Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
  22|                       CEMETERY
  23|                                   One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the
  24|                       North and South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting
  25|                       regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment
  26|                       and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  27|                                    As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our
  28|                       residents and fellow Town employees as we persevere through these difficult times.
  29|                                   "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the
  30|                       resources of the Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or
  31|                       exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella
  32|                       of public works"

   1|                                                   Lunenburg Public Library
   2|                        During FY 2014 and FY 2015 we continued our efforts in several areas: technology, collection
   3|                        development and programs. We completed an extensive overhaul of our technical infrastructure which
   4|                       included creating an entirely new website (www.lunenburglibrary.org). We have had great feedback on
   5|                       the website and are adding new aspects and information to it all the time. We included a link so the public
   6|                       can easily give us feedback or ask a question of the Library staff. The LPL also "virtualized" all of our
   7|                        public computers. This has been a huge success in both gaining efficiency - we decreased the time it
   8|                       takes to open the Library by 50%. By using Computing virtualization devices we decreased our energy
   9|                        consumption by 70% and also decreased our computer maintenance cost by about 600% as we do not
  10|                        need to purchase, maintain, or replace CPUs any longer for our public computers. We also added a new
  11|                        Wi-Fi access point to increase signal strength and access. The Library was also part of a State-wide
  12|                       installation of fiber optic cable for improved access to the internet for rural communities. We partnered
  13|                       with the Town's Green Committee Task Force to migrate our exterior lighting to LED for more energy
  14|                       efficiency. In FY2016 we hope to do the same with our interior lighting.
  15|                       In terms of services and programs we have continued our weekly PC Tech Sessions with Doug Pulsifer
  16|                        and also added a new bi-weekly E-Book Tech Session so you can learn how to operate and download all
  17|                        kinds of E-content. The LPL has begun to reach out to other community organizations and partner with
  18|                       them to bring services and programs to Lunenburg through the library. One example of this was
  19|                       providing one-on-one help with navigating new health insurance choices. We also help to promote the
  20|                       sale of the Friendly Seniors May Baskets which help support their organization and we hosted the Girl
  21|                        Scouts car-wash and many scout meetings. The Eagle Scouts worked on a face lift of our beautiful garden
  22|                       at the back of the Library. The Friends hosted their Cultural Horizon series of events, this time focusing
  23|                       on the Scandinavian culture. Sandy Altucher, our Digital Services Librarian has begun an ongoing
  24|                       weekly Writers Group - the one class/group very quickly became two due to the number of attendees.
  25|                       During the winter that WAS the Library had some very challenging times with broken pipes, damaged
  26|                       ceilings and rooms. Our huge as in HUGE thanks to the DPW and the Fire Department for their quick
  27|                        action on that most horrible day for their quick response which saved us immeasurable damage to our
  28|                       collections and the building. We just, as May 21st                       , completed the repairs to the Library. FY 16 should
  29|                        bring us a new burner and building management system which will help us avoid similar disasters in the
  30|                        future.
  31|                        We said goodbye to our extraordinary Library Page, Jeff Dike, who went off to Boston and the
  32|                        Wentworth Institute of Technology. New Library Pages, Shelby Scorse and Joe Rager were hired and
  33|                        quickly learned the ropes at the LPL. One of the biggest, perhaps earth-shattering changes was the
  34|                       retirement of Kate McCarron, our Teen/YA Librarian for 23 years!! Kate's creative and heart-filled
  35|                       approached to job will be sorely missed.
  36|                       In terms of the numbers FY2014 (FY2015 not available yet):
  37|                              • added over 9,000 items to the collection
  38|                              • circulated over 98,000 items
  39|                                   interlibrary-loaned 22,628 items
  40|                              • provided 313 programs — an increase of 47% over the previous year

===PAGE 59=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                                 SEWER COMMISSION
   2|                         The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006.
   3|                         At the May 2013 Town Election, Carl Luck and Mike Nault were re-elected for three year terms and the officers
   4|                         voted in were: Carl Luck -continuing as Chairman, Jay Simeone- Vice-Chairman and Dave MacDonald - Clerk.
   5|                          The other members are Butch Bilotta and Mike Nault. The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of
   6|                         overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and implementing rules,
   7|                         regulations and policies. The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the operational side of the
   8|                         system. Under the Town Charter responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the
   9|                         sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a
  10|                         sewer treatment plant. Approximately 76% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the
  11|                         other 24% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  12|                         There are currently 730 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 390 that have access to town
  13|                         sewer but are not yet connected. During Fiscal Year 2014, forty new accounts connected to town sewer. The
  14|                         Commission billed out $656,440 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $53,730 of prior year delinquencies added to
  15|                         the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Sixteen properties with access to sewer were assessed privilege
  16|                         fees that had not originally been assessed betterments. Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the
  17|                         Town on the original sewer project.
  18|                         The Sewer Commission was very proud to present a balanced budget during the FY15 budget process during
  19|                          FY14. This means that for the second time it was not necessary to transfer any monies from Retained Earnings
  20|                         to operate the Sewer Division of the DPW, which had been the case every year prior to FY14. The goal of the
  21|                         Sewer Commission is to maintain a balanced budget going forward and to build up the Retained Earnings so that
  22|                         it may used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating efficiency of the system.
  23|                         In the spring of 2013, the Sewer Commission solicited proposals to convert from in-house service of the 10
  24|                         pumping stations and sewer infrastructure to having an outside contractor perform the service and maintenance.
  25|                          The Sewer Commission selected Small Water Systems Services of Littleton for a one-year contract with the
  26|                         potential for up to three years starting in August 2013. Corey Dowd who had served as the Sewer Technician
  27|                         moved over to the Highway Division of the DPW. As the fiscal year 2014 drew to a close, the Commission has
  28|                         been very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with Small Water Systems Services and has
  29|                         already put into motion to add to their responsibilities the maintenance of the 10 generators housed in our
  30|                         pumping stations with the next contract year. The Commission also worked hard over the course of FY14 with
  31|                         Small Water to explore the costs and benefits of installing SCADA systems in the pumping stations which
  32|                          allows for remote checking of alarms, hopefully reducing the number of call outs and expense.
  33|                         The Sewer Commission had several large projects throughout FY14. In September 2013, the revision and
  34|                         implementation of the Sewer Use Regulations became effective. With this document, all bylaws, policies, rules
  35|                         and regulations were compiled into one document. One of the new policies developed and utilized this year was
  36|                         the Illegal Connection Policy for use when a property connects to the town sewer without obtaining the proper
  37|                         permits. A minimum fee of $5000 will be levied to the property owner along with back usage charges if the
  38|                         Commission learns of an illegal connection. The Commission offered "amnesty" to anyone who knowingly was
  39|                         connected and was not paying a sewer bill from the inception of the policy through October 2013. In
  40|                         Lunenburg, the water and sewer is billed separately, so a resident would know that they are illegally connected
  41|                         if they do not have a septic system and do not receive a quarterly sewer bill. The Commission would encourage
  42|                         any resident in that situation come forward to rectify it versus having the illegal connection discovered and be
  43|                          subject to substantial fines. The Commission also worked to enforce the Grease Trap Regulation for all food
  44|                          service establishments that have or should have a grease trap or grease interceptor. Annual permit fees were
  45|                         assessed in October 2013 based on the establishment's level of compliance and the reporting requirements
  46|                         clearly communicated. Lunenburg has 19 grease trap establishments. This regulation will continue to be
  47|                         strongly enforced as fats, oils and grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance
  48|                          costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal,
  49|                                                                                               59

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|             individual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases
   2|             everyone's costs. The Commission understands that education is an important aspect of their role and will
   3|             continue to provide information to their customers to keep the sewer system running economically and
   4|             efficiently by reducing the disposal of fats, oil and grease into the system to avoid blockages. Whether on sewer
   5|             or septic, grease should not be put down the drain. Another enemy of the sewer system is "disposable wipes"
   6|             which do not bio-degrade and bring our pumps to a halt. Nothing but organic waste and toilet paper should be
   7|            put into the sewer system. In July 2013 when the State required that Leominster implement a "sewer bank fee"
   8|            for the mandated Inflow/Infiltration remediation, they passed along that request to those towns which have flow
   9|            processed by Leominster. The Lunenburg Sewer Commission adopted the I/I Sewer bank fee effective 7/30/13,
  10|             to be collected along with the standard connection fee. The Commission, working with Weston & Sampson
  11|             Engineering, continued their work to find sources of 1/I (Inflow/Infiltration) by performing a house-to-house
  12|            inspection in the Whalom area in the Spring of 2014, exactly what the Sewer Bank is designed for; to ensure
  13|             that the Commonwealth of Massachusetts towns work to eliminate extraneous sources flowing into sewer
  14|             treatment systems.
  15|             A major goal of the Commission is to increase sewer revenues by adding new customers both within the
  16|             currently sewered areas as well as identified needs areas per the 2010 Town-funded Comprehensive Wastewater
  17|            Management Plan (CWMP). During FY14, the Commission worked closely with several resident groups
  18|            interested in sewer extensions to connect to public sewer. A group of seven residents on Lancaster Ave. worked
  19|            successfully with the Commission on the installation of a privately-funded sewer extension from Whiting St.
  20|            and accepted by the Town on November 7, 2013. A second group came forward with a request for the
  21|             Commission's assistance to extend the sewer from Prospect Avenue to the Pine Grove Road area, as well as the
  22|             Sunset Avenue area. This last extension would serve 42 parcels and eliminate an environmental concern for
  23|             Whalom Lake. A second group came forward with a request for the Commission's assistance to extend the
  24|             sewer from Prospect Ave. to the Pine Grove Rd. area, as well as the Sunset Ave. area. This last extension would
  25|             serve 42 parcels and eliminate an environmental concern for Whalom Lake. Another area of town that had
  26|             come forward in the past with interest in connecting to sewer (Pratt St. & Rennie St.) was included in the same
  27|            application to the State. Both areas had two thirds support of the residents, so the Commission contracted with
  28|             Wright Pierce, who had worked with the town on the 2010 CWMP, to put together the SRF Proposal application
  29|            (PEF). It was submitted to the State to apply for low-interest loans from the State Revolving Fund (SRF) in
  30|             August 2013. The proposal attained a sufficient rating to be assured of an SRF loan and the Commission
  31|            presented the project at the spring Town Meeting in May 2014 and obtained approval to move the project
  32|            forward. These extensions as proposed would have little impact to the system costs but would improve the
  33|             bottom line by providing more revenue to the Sewer Enterprise Fund. Also during FY14 an additional private
  34|             extension was brought before the Commission to connect a residence on Highland Avenue to the sewer main on
  35|             Oak Avenue.
  36|             The Commission continued to work diligently with the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen and the City of Fitchburg
  37|            to complete an updated Inter-Municipal Agreement (IMA), which went into effect 12/15/13, include converting
  38|             the basis by which we pay Fitchburg to the amount of outflow as measured by in-ground flow meters at the town
  39|            line. A major accomplishment of the new IMA was the elimination of a use "adder" which was a significant
  40|             portion of the cost. These two issues have reduced the cost of sending flow to Fitchburg, as the switch to the
  41|             meter basis went into effect in February 2014. The new IMA also includes terms for Lunenburg to pay for its
  42|             portion of Fitchburg's proposed John Fitch Highway improvement project in order to secure a guarantee of
  43|            access for future projected needs. This represents the first time Lunenburg has had to pay directly for capacity
  44|             and during the remainder of FY14 the Commission worked to create the new Reserve Capacity Fee policy,
  45|             which went into effect at the end of FY14. This policy ensures a fair sharing of the cost of purchasing additional
  46|             capacity to new users. Chairman Carl Luck and past Chairman and current Board of Selectmen liaison, Bob
  47|             Ebersole worked tirelessly with Fitchburg officials to get this agreement renewed with terms that are more
  48|             favorable to the Town of Lunenburg. The Sewer Commission is grateful to them for their hard work and
  49|             perseverance in getting this important agreement between Fitchburg and Lunenburg finalized. The
  50|             Commission also wishes to thank Joe Jordan, who stepped down as the director of the Fitchburg Wastewater
  51|             Division in June 2014 for his hard work and wishes him the best in his new endeavor.
  52|                                                                                               60

   1|                                                                           TECHNOLOGY
   2|             The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through
   3|             the use of technology. The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to
   4|             communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks, organize, store, and share information, and
   5|             generally conduct business more effectively. We also provide the necessary infrastructure,
   6|             equipment, software, and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when
   7|             interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   8|             Our staff has remained the same this year, but our network administrator Josh Branham has taken
   9|             on a full time position following graduating from Fitchburg State.
  10|             Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  11|                   • Hired in December, 2012
  12|                   • Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA
  13|                          and Belchertown Public Schools
  14|             Josh Branham - Network Administrator
  15|                   • Over six years working for the town and school system
  16|                   • Stepping into a full time role this year having successfully completed his Bachelor's
  17|                          Degree in Computer Information Systems - Congratulations Josh!
  18|             Alex Smith - Systems Administration (part time)
  19|                   • Over three years working for the town and school system
  20|                          Currently half way through his senior year at Fitchburg State majoring in Computer
  21|                          Science
  22|             Along with serving as helpdesk for all users' daily technology issues and the usual maintenance
  23|             and scheduled replacement of the town's and schools' technology assets, the IT Department has
  24|             been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year. The following
  25|             examples are among the more noteworthy improvements that IT has implemented.
  26|             We have continued our efforts to virtualize all server hardware via VMware vSphere. Lunenburg
  27|             High School, Middle School, and Primary School, as well as the Town Hall and Public Safety
  28|             complex servers have all been converted to virtual machines. This conversion provides greatly
  29|             enhanced management and backup, while creating a hardware-independent environment. If a
  30|             physical server fails, the virtual servers can be moved to almost any available hardware,
  31|             simplifying disaster recovery.
  32|             An enterprise-style Wi-Fi solution has been deployed at the Public Safety Complex. Replacing
  33|             older, consumer grade equipment has given us universal coverage within the complex. The
  34|             system also offers an open guest network enabling visitors to access the internet, while
  35|             simultaneously providing a secure connection to internal networks and services for our police
  36|             officers, fire fighters, EMS personnel and administration.
  37|             The technology department also assisted with the implementation of fleet management software
  38|             for the Department of Public Works. Keeping track of use and maintenance schedules is
  39|             absolutely critical for an organization which relies on a large and varied fleet of vehicles to
  40|                                                                                               62

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 9 lines)
   1|                                                                                      TECHNOLOGY
   2|                        perform the ongoing tasks of keeping Lunenburg's physical infrastructure safe and functional for
   3|                        all town residents and visitors.
   4|                        We continue to strive to leverage limited resources to improve efficiency wherever possible. I
   5|                        am very happy to have stepped into this role, and I couldn't be more pleased with the level of
   6|                        skill, hard work and dedication of my staff, Josh and Alex. I know that because of them we will
   7|                        continue to improve the technology landscape in Lunenburg while maintaining the positive
   8|                        reputation they helped to garner over the past several years.
   9|                        Submitted by Steve Malandrinos, IT Director

   1|                                                                                 BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|                          The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter Library Building on the corner of
   3|                          Massachusetts Avenue and Leominster Road. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the
   4|                          hall on the right. We can be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   5|                          The Board of Health, with the assistance of its agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health,
   6|                          continued its mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2014;
   7|                         reviewing Title V Inspection Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance
   8|                         investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health related issues.
   9|                          Homeland Security planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other Town
  10|                          Departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner
  11|                         towns in our region.
  12|                          This year, we processed applications for septic permit lot tests, new construction permits, new
  13|                          wells and repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its
  14|                          vigilance with regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance
  15|                          complaints and its general mandate to protect the environment and ground waters of the
  16|                          Commonwealth.
  17|                          The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our health agents, the
  18|                          Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous
  19|                          clinics are provided throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide vaccine
  20|                         inoculations, blood pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and dental screening. Fluoride
  21|                          treatment and dental checks of our youngsters in the school system are provided by the Nashoba
  22|                          dental staff.
  23|                          The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito
  24|                          Control monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases
  25|                          such as EEE and West Nile Virus. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so
  26|                          by emailing to: cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, or completing a request on their
  27|                          webpage at: http://www.cmmcp.org.
  28|                          With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents
  29|                          recovering from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  30|                          Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  31|                          Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the
  32|                          Citizens of Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your
  33|                          service.
  34|                          Contact the Board office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

  12|                                                               Nashoba annually mails out and receives applications from both

  15|                                                  Most licensees are inspected at a minimum twice a year. Where
