===PAGE 74=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                                                        FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|                      The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
   3|                 dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
   4|                                                              the citizens of the Town of Lunenburg.
   5|                          In 2014 the Fire Department responded to 1957 calls for service as follows:
   6|                          Fires & Hazardous Conditions                               521                     Emergency Medical                 914
   7|                          Inspections                                                458                     Other                             64
   8|                          Fire Department staffing today is five career staff and forty- forty-five on call firefighters and
   9|                mergency medical technicians. The station is statted Monday through Friday trom b a.m. - 6 p.m
  10|                lowever, on weeknights and weekends all of our personnel respond from home on an "on call" basis
  11|              This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the station, pick up
  12|              the appropriate equipment and then respond to the call. This staffing model has not seen a substantial
  13|              change in over 30 years, even though we have experienced a nearly 400% increase in calls for service
  14|              during that period. While we have added four additional full time personnel in that time to bolster our
  15|              day time response, it has only resulted in a 12% increase in the time the station is manned. We still rely
  16|               on our on call personnel to be the primary responders 64% of the week.
  17|                          2014 was our busiest year to date, and given the growth occurring in town, I can only anticipate
  18|              further increases in service requests. While the commitment shown by our call force is nothing short of
  19|              amazing, the ever increasing call volume is straining our ability to provide the high level of service
  20|              townspeople have come to expect. These factors lead me to continue to call for 24 hour staffing in the
  21|              station. Sufficient funding to maintain two Firefighter/EMT's in the station 24 hours a day, 7 days a
  22|              week would allow either the first ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond immediately. Not
  23|              only would this reduce our response times to critical emergencies, it would also reduce the "wear and
  24|               tear" on our call staff. This coverage would in no way reduce the need for our on call personnel; it would
  25|               only supplement their response and reduce response times.
  26|                          Our firefighters continue to train for the myriad of situations which can be encountered even in a
  27|              town such as ours. Today's firefighter must be skilled in not only firefighting, but medical care,
  28|               hazardous materials mitigation and technical rescue. Our EMT's must continue to stay abreast of the
  29|              latest treatment protocols and advances in lifesaving skills. Personnel train regularly both here at the
  30|              station and at classes sponsored by other agencies. While they are compensated for some of the training,
  31|              the vast majority is done on their own time. To give an example, a new Firefighter/EMT must attend over
  32|              340 hours of basic firefighting and emergency medical technician training. Once they are certified they
  33|              must complete over 40 hours of continuing education and refresher training every two years, just for their
  34|              EMT. Operators of fire apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of training in pump and aerial
  35|              operations, hydraulics calculations and vehicle operation.
  36|                           Several of our personnel are members of Regional Technical Rescue units, providing services for
  37|              Confined Space and Cave in Rescue, as well as Underwater Search and Recovery. They stand ready to
  38|              respond as needed throughout Northern Worcester County.
  39|                           2014 continued to be an active year for our fire prevention efforts. By law we are required to
  40|              inspect smoke and carbon monoxide d Personnel also continue to provide fire safety education to not only
  41|              children in our schools but adults as well. Other fire safety initiatives undertaken during 2014 include:
  42|                                                                                               74

===PAGE 75=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                   - Implementation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $4,900 to assist with
   2|                                         fire safety education in both the schools and with senior citizens. This program, known as
   3|                                         SAFE and Senior SAFE was renewed in late 2014 with an additional $7,200 in grant funding.
   4|                                   - Participation in the senior center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides
   5|                                         buckets of walkway sand for senior citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in
   6|                                         the winter months. In partnership with this program, our Fire Prevention Officer Captain
   7|                                         James Ricci would deliver the sand to the residents and when possible, inspect their homes
   8|                                         for smoke and carbon monoxide detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci
   9|                                          would install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant. Capt. Ricci has
  10|                                         installed well over 50 smoke and carbon monoxide detectors for our seniors since the
  11|                                         inception of this program.
  12|                                         Lieutenant Mark Bursch completed the Fire Academy's "Public Life Safety Educator"
  13|                                         Program. This provides us another certified educator to provide fire and life safety
  14|                                         educational programs to the public.
  15|                                   Several members are also Certified First Aid and CPR Instructors and provide training to both
  16|                       citizens and town employees.
  17|                                   Our department continues to actively pursue grant funding opportunities as they occur, though
  18|                       those opportunities are becoming scarcer.
  19|                                   The new ambulance funded under the FY '14 Capital Plan was received in late June and placed in
  20|                       service as Rescue 1. The unit has seen steady service since then and will service the community for many
  21|                       years to come. The former Rescue 1 (2008 vintage) was re-designated Rescue 2 and serves as our back-
  22|                       up ambulance. The 2004 vintage unit was traded in.
  23|                                    The replacement for Engine 1 approved in the FY '15 Capital Plan has been ordered and is
  24|                       expected to arrive in May 2015. This new unit will become our "first due" engine and will replace a 1991
  25|                       model.
  26|                                    Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern. FY'15 has seen a significant increase in
  27|                       vehicle maintenance costs, particularly with the older apparatus. The replacements of the above units will
  28|                       hopefully decrease some of our vehicle maintenance costs. However, two of our front line units (Engine
  29|                       4 and Ladder 1) are over 20 years old and showing their age through increasing maintenance costs and
  30|                       "out of service" time. These units are included the department's 5 Year Capital Plan for replacement in
  31|                       FY '17 and FY '18 respectively and it is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely
  32|                       replacement. The high reliability required of front line emergency apparatus becomes increasingly
  33|                       expensive and difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.
  34|                                    Firefighters Matthew Benoit, Michael Byrne and Timothy Downey graduated from the
  35|                        Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's Call/Volunteer Training Program. This intensive 6 month
  36|                       program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today's challenging environments. All
  37|                       newly hired firefighters are required to complete this program within one year of hire. In addition,
  38|                       Firefighters Benoit, Downey, P. Andrew Duquette and James Reynolds obtained their certification as
  39|                       Emergency Medical Technicians.
  40|                                   In October 2014 two Lunenburg Firefighters were recognized at the Annual Firefighter of the
  41|                       Year Awards, held at the JFK Library in Boston. This was the first time that any of our firefighters have
  42|                       received this recognition in the 25 year history of the awards. Captain James Ricci received the Norman
  43|                       Knight Award for his outstanding efforts in Fire Prevention and Safety throughout the Community.
  44|                       Firefighter Austin Flagg received recognition for his rescue of a badly injured resident from a structure
  45|                       fire in January.
  46|                                                                                               75

   1|                         In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
   2|              outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to
   3|              impress me every day. I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for
   4|              their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in
   5|              the daily course of town business. My thanks also go out to the Citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing
   6|              support of public safety. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the services you expect
   7|              trom us.

   6|                                                                                                                  POLICE
   7|                                                                                                                                                  LICE
   8|                         "Police must secure the willing co-operation of the public in voluntary observance of the law to be able to secure and
   9|                           maintain the respect of the public."
  10|                           Sir Robert Peel
  11|                                                                                          Mission Statement
  12|                         The mission of the Lunenburg Police Department is defined by a more advanced style of policing that emphasizes personal
  13|                         service and community problem resolution, and accentuates the uncompromising integrity, trust, respect, and accountability, of
  14|                         the police officers who are committed to providing a safe and secure environment for every citizen.
  15|                           The men and women of the Lunenburg Police Department are required to accept, support, and carry out this mission in a
  16|                           manner consistent with our core values and with distinction, given all practical resources, in the interest of the protection
  17|                                                                of life and property, preventing crime, and preserving peace.
  18|                                                                                             Core Values
  19|                          Courage-we, the men and women of the Lunenburg Police Department, accept as our primary obligation to be
  20|                         physically, morally, and mentally strong against the challenges we bear as law enforcement officers; always standing firm
  21|                         in the face of danger, confronting threats without apprehension, placing the safety and security of others before our own
  22|                         with honor.
  23|                                                                                               77

===PAGE 78=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|            Integrity—we possess an uncompromising sense of rectitude with undying respect and concern for humanity while
   2|            safeguarding the dignity of all people; we are committed to the highest level of moral principles and ethics in our actions
   3|            and appearance worthy of the public trust; never placing ourselves above the law and always inspiring the confidence of
   4|            our community whether on or off duty.
   5|            Commitment—we will work together as a team, promoting leadership within the organization, among our peers and
   6|            throughout the community; we are committed to faithfully meeting the demands of our profession, achieving
   7|            organizational goals that serve the public, and performing all of our duties to the best of our abilities with dedication and
   8|            determination; preserving the reputation and morale of the police department; always accepting responsibility for our
   9|            actions, always consistent, fair and impartial; treating all citizens with dignity and respect, and always concerned with the
  10|            welfare and quality of life for all people.

   1|                                                     Incidents, Arrests, Calls For Service, & Crime Rates
   2|               Calls for service have increased by 13.3 percent over last year, nearly twice the amount predicted last year, and 35.7%
   3|               over the past five years. More than 3000 man hours were used this year responding to calls.
   4|              Reportable incidents and Field Interviews are all crimes that did not originate as arrests, and would need to be investigated
   5|               and reported on or an interview with a suspect or victim was necessary and reported grew by 8.3% over last year.
   6|              Incidents and Field Interviews have risen over the past five years by 80.8% with an average annual rate of 12%.
   7|               Officers expend an enormous amount of time investigating, following up, and reporting on incidents. This year the man
   8|               hours to complete just reports for incidents and field interviews exceeded 1700.

  35|                                                                            Arrests-PC'S-Summons
  36|               Arrests, Protective Custody and Criminal Complaint Summons have increased by 47.3% over last year and 78.6% over
  37|              the past five years with an average annual rate of 15%. To assure the safety of the officers and detainees department
  38|               policy requires a minimum of two officers for all arrests and bookings. The average arrests including, processing at the
  39|               scene of arrest, transporting to the police lock-up, booking, processing evidence, completing arrest reports and related
  40|               documents, and then transporting the prisoner to court takes approximately four (4) hours.
  41|               Many time two officers will be scheduled per shift. When an arrest commits both officers additional help will be called in
  42|               at the overtime rate to handle calls.
  43|               Violent crime rose to 6% above last year with Simple assaults responsible most of the increase. Property crimes increased
  44|              by 28%. Burglaries went down with Shoplifting/Larceny, Forgery and Fraud driving the increase. Drug and weapons
  45|               violations cause crimes against society to increase 63%. Disorderly conduct, alcohol related crime, driving under the
  46|              influence and trespassing topped the Group B crimes with a 64% increase.
  47|               The rise in crime can be attributed to many factors such as the unemployment rate. The increase in drug related crimes
  48|              could be due to a sharp rise in opioid addiction. Modes of transportation have increased. The geographical location places
  49|                                                                                               80

   2|                               account for a significant part of the crime rate. Other factors include increase in population, effective strength of law
   3|                               enforcement, administrative and investigative emphasis of law enforcement; areas that could be improved with increased
   4|                               staffing levels.

  29|                                                                                             Current Issues
  30|                               As of December 20, 2015 the 3 pm to 7 pm, Monday thru Friday, Desk Watch officer position has been discontinued.
  31|                               Lack of funding will not allow me to keep the public safety building open during those hours.
  32|                               As I have continually emphasized; leaving a public safety building un-manned in a community such as ours raises
  33|                               fundamental questions, irrespective of fiscal restraint, about the police department's and fire department's day to day
  34|                               operations, the services demanded by the public, and the safety and security of the building and the community.
  35|                               The importance of the desk officer position with respect to the service it represents is evident. A sample of data revealed
  36|                               that that in a 35 day period, from the 2"d of September to October 21st, 35 days, 709 calls were logged and the public
  37|                               visited the lobby for police or fire service 414 times. The day shift desk officer investigated 136 complaints in the lobby
  38|                               over the past year; larcenies, missing persons, violations of restraining orders, served harassment orders, restraining
  39|                               orders, motor vehicle accidents, motor vehicle complaints, neighbor disputes, medical emergencies, child abuse annoying
  40|                                                                                               81

===PAGE 82=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|            phone calls, threats, trespassing, vandalism, sexual assault, processed 61 firearms licenses, registered 6 sex offenders just
   2|            to name a few. The 3 pm to 7 pm shift which was cut back in December, logged nearly half again that of the daytime desk
   3|            officer.
   4|            Staffing issues never seem to go away. The town has far outgrown its police department. The increased demand for
   5|            service with inadequate staffing has a direct effect on our ability to decrease overtime, reduces officer initiated activities,
   6|            and compromises our crime clearance rate. The demand for service has increased 35.7% over the last five years. Calls for
   7|            service, arrests, criminal complaints, and incidents continue to increase dramatically. Patrolling the streets to increase
   8|            visibility, to reduce the fear of crime, or prevent crime has become problematic again this year because the work load has
   9|            increased. Again this year officers had to be called in or were held over their regular scheduled shifts to meet the work
  10|           load demand. In the first six months of Fiscal 2015 officers were called in for various reasons 104 times, a 44.4%
  11|           increase over last year. As I have said in the past, growth will continue to bridge the gap between staffing levels and the
  12|            workload. Services will never rise to an acceptable level without a meaningful increase in the amount of police officers
  13|            we employ.
  14|            Emergency response will never change. The police will always have to respond to emergencies, and the community will
  15|            have to provide an adequate level of police staffing to perform this function with respect to reasonable response times,
  16|            with respect to the safety of the public and with the safety of police officers in mind, and not the least of which to reduce
  17|            exposure to civil liability.
  18|            Non-emergency response where officers respond to calls and collect information and evidence and take statements
  19|            necessary to solve crimes, will also continue to climb in numbers with social change and growth.
  20|            Quality of life issues come under the classification of officer initiated calls. An example of this level of policing is traffic
  21|            enforcement and crime prevention. The public expects the same service with both these levels of law enforcement and
  22|            these are the services that suffer the most from budget cuts and inadequate staffing. When the police have no time to
  23|            properly investigate crimes, engage the community to solve problems, or provide quick emergency response because of
  24|            the increased demands placed on individual officers due to poor staffing levels, then the community will quickly see the
  25|            police as ineffective. The community will be less and less interested in supporting a service that has no value.
  26|            Thank you again this year for allowing me to serve as your Chief of Police. I fully appreciate and I value, and I thank you
  27|            for the support the town has shown the police department. I humbly ask for your uncompromising commitment going
  28|            forward as I seek funding for additional staffing and desk service at the public safety building so that I can provide the
  29|            services that Lunenburg needs, that Lunenburg deserves, and that Lunenburg is entitled to.
  30|            Despite all these challenges we face, Lunenburg is still a safe place to reside or visit. I can assure you the men and women
  31|            of the Lunenburg Police Department stand by our promise to carry out our mission and to realize our vision; to give the
  32|            best service, to guarantee the greatest security and provide safest environment for every citizen.
  33|            Respectfully submitted,
  34|             Chief James P Marino
  35|            Chief James P. Marino

   1|                                                                                  CAPITAL PLANNING
   2|                         Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal
   3|                         Year (FY) Plan and a Capital Improvement Plan. These plans are to be presented to the Town
   4|                         Manager.
   5|                          As noted in the past reports, the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety,
   6|                         Need, Crisis, Cost Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Capital Planning Committee has to
   7|                         review each request under these six elements. Not only do they have to determine that the
   8|                         request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize the level that meets the criteria.
   9|                          Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly due to funding. The result of the
  10|                         proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and protection of the public, the
  11|                         upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  12|                         At the Annual Town Meeting May 2014, the following items, totaling $ 1,114,152, were
  13|                         presented in the FY15 Plan.

   1|                                                                         PLANNING BOARD
   2|              The Planning Board welcomed new member Kenneth Chenis and returning member Matthew
   3|               Allison. Emerick Bakaysa resigned from the Board after serving some twenty years. Marion M.
   4|               Benson retired from her position as Planning Director after serving on the Planning Board since
   5|               1983, and assuming the position of Planning Director in 2005. The Planning Director's position
   6|               was replaced with a Land Use Director position to coordinate the activities of all boards and
   7|              departments involved in land use/development in town. Adam R. Burney, previously serving as
   8|               Acting Director of Development & Inspectional Services for the Town of Auburn, was hired as
   9|               Land Use Director.
  10|               The Board signed eleven (11) ANR plans (subdivision of lots for separate building lots).
  11|               Emerald Place at Lake Whalom continued construction according to the approved plan with
  12|              three different housing styles.
  13|               Tri Town Landing, Youngs Road was awarded some tax credit funding and hopes to begin
  14|               construction on Building 4. Buildings 1, 2, and 3 are occupied as rentals and all ninety-nine (99)
  15|              units have been rented.
  16|               Stone Farm Estates, Massachusetts Avenue is still being constructed to completion.
  17|               Highfield Village, Northfield Road is still in review. Several extensions have been given.
  18|               Whitetail Crossing plans on requesting town acceptance of the roadway interior at May 2015
  19|              Town Meeting
  20|               Sequoia Drive and Whites Woods Phase III are building upon request.
  21|               At May Town Meeting the Town passed an article to add the Registered Marijuana Dispensary
  22|               Bylaw to the Zoning Bylaws. This bylaw governs the placement of these facilities in suitable
  23|              locations, minimizing the adverse impact by regulating the siting, design, placement, and
  24|              security of these facilities.
  25|              In conjunction with the Montachusett Regional Planning Commission, a Housing Production
  26|               Plan was designed. This plan, approved by both the Board of Selectmen and the Planning Board,
  27|               will aid Lunenburg in guiding housing development and can influence the type, amount, and
  28|              location of affordable housing. In January 2015, the plan will be forwarded to the State for
  29|              approval.
  30|               A Village Center District Bylaw was crafted to be presented as a warrant article at May 2015
  31|               Town Meeting. The creation of this new district in the Center is intended to encourage growth
  32|              that creates a dynamic active environment while retaining the area's existing character.
  33|               Chair Joanna Bilotta-Simeone is the Board's representative to the Montachusett Regional
  34|               Planning Commission and the Capital Planning Committee. Nathan Lockwood is the

   1|                                                              ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2|              The Zoning Board of Appeals heard many petitions during calendar year 2014. There were several
   3|              applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board
   4|              meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month on an "as needed" basis.
   5|              The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Donald F. Bowen, Chairman, Raymond Beal,
   6|              Vice Chairman, James Besarkarski, Alfred Gravelle, Hans Wentrup and Associate members, David Blatt,
   7|              Paul Doherty and Sheila Lumi.                  Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning
   8|              Board.
   9|              The functions of the Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  10|                   1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in
  11|                          the By-laws.
  12|                    2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-
  13|                          laws.
  14|                    3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts
  15|                          General Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain
  16|                          a permit or enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the
  17|                          Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  18|                   4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General
  19|                          Laws, Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits
  20|                          submitted to the Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.
  21|                          These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  22|              The Office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
  23|              Avenue. If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146
  24|              X 421 during regular business hours. Zoning Board applications are available in the office of the Town
  25|              Clerk and online at www.Lunenburgonline.com.

===PAGE 92=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                  Superintendent's Message
   2|              I am honored to report to you another on another exciting and productive year for the Lunenburg
   3|              Public Schools. Certainly the start of construction on the new middle-high school is a highlight.
   4|              It has been inspiring to see the changes to the campus landscape and watch the building and one
   5|              of three new fields be constructed. Site enabling began summer 2014 as local contractor Powell
   6|              Stone and Gravel started removing topsoil and trees so construction could get underway. We all
   7|              experienced a sense of loss with the removal of most of the trees from the front lawn, but that
   8|              feeling was soon replaced with excitement as the footprint of the new building was made distinct
   9|              and the grading, leveling, irrigation and sod installation on the front field progressed. The
  10|              ceremonial groundbreaking was held October 18, 2014 in conjunction with Homecoming. What
  11|              a proud and glorious day that was for our community! Certainly the best speeches of that day
  12|              were delivered by students Lauren Basque, President of the Class of 2016, the last class to
  13|              graduate from the currently Lunenburg High School and Brendan Tobin, President of the Class of
  14|              2017, the first class to graduate from the new school. Planning for the next milestone ceremony,
  15|              the "Topping Off" is underway. This ceremony is a steelworker's tradition for placing the last
  16|              beam.
  17|              The building project progressed from site enabling to the pouring of footings and frost walls.
  18|              Work on the front field was completed in mid-October. The sod and grass require at least one
  19|              growing season before use and the intent is to bring the field on line no later than Fall 2015.
  20|              School athletes and Town youth program participants and their coaches have cooperated well to
  21|              share the limited field space that remains during construction with all soccer programs needing to
  22|              go off-site. I want to recognize and thank Athletic Director Peter McCauliff and members of the
  23|              Lunenburg youth sports groups for their efforts in scheduling the limited fields.
  24|              Structural steel arrived at the site in December and the first column was placed December 8,
  25|              2014. It was remarkable how quickly the erection of the steel progressed, and by January the
  26|              high school wing and auditorium steel were in place and the high school wing wrapped.                                                          The
  27|              temporary tent enclosure with temporary heating and lighting services allows work to continue in
  28|              winter conditions. The historic snow fall and frigid temperature in the final days of January and
  29|              through February slowed, but did not halt progress. In the final week of February, as I write this

   1|                                                                                       School Facilities
   2|                                                                                             2014-2015
   3|                       Site work began on the Lunenburg Middle/High School project in July 2014. Shawmut
   4|                       Construction Management is executing the project with oversight by Joslin, Lesser & Associates
   5|                       and the School Building Committee. Foundation work began in September and was completed in
   6|                       early December. The building's steel structure began to be installed in December 2014 and is
   7|                       expected to be complete by the end of March 2015
   8|                       The FY15 capital plan approved upgrades to the front end controllers of the Building
   9|                       Management Systems at Lunenburg Primary School at a total cost of $14,000. This work was
  10|                       awarded to Control Technologies and was completed by September 2014. Also funded in the
  11|                       FY15 Capital Plan was $138,000 to abate asbestos at Turkey Hill Middle School. The asbestos
  12|                       abatement work was bid in May 2014 but came in at $175,000 so no work was completed during
  13|                       the summer of 2014. The scope of the work has been reduced and is expected to be rebid in April
  14|                       2015.
  15|                       The focus of the school maintenance effort for FY15 continued to concentrate on preventative
  16|                       maintenance of heat and ventilation systems and energy conservation.
  17|                       The Town of Lunenburg successfully applied as a Green Community in 2013. Projects that were
  18|                       completed for Turkey Hill Middle School in the summer of 2014 include replacement of the
  19|                       building's climate control air compressor with a smaller unit and installation of variable speed
  20|                       drives on the building heating circulation pumps. Lunenburg Primary School energy efficiency
  21|                       measures planned included lighting upgrades, however they were not funded. Efforts during the
  22|                       year were directed at finding a more cost effective approach to upgrade the lighting systems.
  23|                       For the FY16 Capital Program, the School Department requested an additional $34,500 for
  24|                       asbestos abatement for Turkey Hill Middle School on a multi-year abatement plan. Also for the
  25|                       Middle School was a request to upgrade the phone system and the intercom system at $22,000
  26|                       each. Requests were also submitted for the District Technology plan at $36,200, a replacement
  27|                       grounds shed for $32,000 and $38,656 to begin to prepare to move the district headquarters to the
  28|                       Turkey Hill Middle School during the summer of 2016. As of the time of this report, it would
  29|                       appear that no funds will be appropriated for any of the FY16 requests.
  30|                       Respectfully submitted,
  31|                       John Londa, Director of Facilities

   1|                                                                 Special Services Department
   2|                                   Town Report - FY14 (data taken from 2013-2014 school year)
   3|                                                Julianna Hanscom, Director of Special Services
   4|                           There are two hundred and seventy-eight students with disabilities receiving special
   5|              education or related services in Lunenburg. This represents 17.8% of our total student population
   6|              of 1,564 students (it was 18.4% last year). This percentage is slightly more than the state average
   7|              of 17%. As of March 1, 2014, the total number of students receiving services outside the district
   8|              was twenty-six. Of the twenty-six students, seven are serviced in Collaborative Placements,
   9|              thirteen are in Day Placements and six are in Residential Placements. During the 2013-2014
  10|              school year (8/28/13 - 6/19/14) seventy-one students identified with a disability received
  11|              accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg.
  12|                           This year as a district, our Accountability and Assistance Level is Level 2 with both
  13|              Turkey Hill Middle and Lunenburg High Schools listed as Level 2. The state report card lists
  14|              Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as "Did Not Meet Target". The district's Students with
  15|               Disabilities progress towards narrowing proficiency gaps [Cumulative Progress and Performance
  16|              Index (CPI): 1-100)] is listed at 54 (it was 50 last year) with the target set for 75. Students with
  17|              Disabilities participated in MCAS this year with the following rates: English Language Arts =
  18|              97%, Mathematics = 98%, and Science = 96%. This data can be found at the DESE
  19|              websitehttp://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=0162
  20|              0000dorgtypecode=5&
  21|                           Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when looking at the
  22|              whole Special Services Department. DESE requires us to collect data for certain Indicators they
  23|              are targeting each year. For our Indicators, the following information is available below and also can be found at:
  24|              http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&
  25|              Indicator 1 - Graduation Rate. For the 2012-2013 school year, the state target for Students with
  26|              Disabilities was 80.0%. Lunenburg surpassed the target with 82.4% and the overall state rate was
  27|              only 67.8% for Students with Disabilities.
  28|              Indicator 2 - Dropout Rate. For the 2012-2013 school year, the state target for Students with
  29|              Disabilities was 4.3%. Lunenburg surpassed the target with 0% and the overall state rate was
  30|              2.9% for Students with Disabilities.
  31|              Indicator 3C - Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS. For the 2013 MCAS,
  32|              Lunenburg Students with Disabilities CPI ratings were as follows:

  25|                         in Special Education Categories. In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no
  26|                         disproportionality found in either case.
  27|                         Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with
  28|                         parental consent to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is
  29|                         reported for all initial evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting
  30|                         year. Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was
  31|                         96.8%.
  32|                         Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by
  33|                         Part C, found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and
  34|                         implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is
  35|                         noted as "Suppressed". Data is suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found
  36|                         eligible for special education services is fewer than 10. In Lunenburg, only 2 students were
  37|                         referred during 2012-2013.
  38|                         Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16
  39|                         and above with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually
  40|                         updated and based upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including
  41|                         courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and
  42|                         annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg was rated 100% in
  43|                         compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 99.2%.
  44|                         Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of
  45|                         students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2011-12 school year and self-reported post-
  46|                         school engagement in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement
  47|                         is defined through three measurements:
  48|                               • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  49|                                                                                               97

   2|                         of leaving high school.
   3|                  • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education
   4|                         or training program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one
   5|                         year of leaving high school.

===PAGE 99=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                      LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                                                                                             2014-2015
   3|                                     The Lunenburg Primary School is a child-centered school devoted to providing quality
   4|                       early childhood education for all students. We are a community of teachers, students, and parents
   5|                       working together to create a safe and respectful environment for children. Current knowledge
   6|                       and research of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices and provides the
   7|                       foundation for learning for all students.
   8|                                     The Lunenburg Primary School services Preschool to Third grade students. The October
   9|                        1, 2013 student count reported to the state was 527 students. Our Preschool program services
  10|                       twelve, three year old students and thirty-three four year old students. We have five Kindergarten
  11|                       classes servicing one hundred and ten students. The class sizes in our Kindergarten programs
  12|                       range from 21-22 students. First grade has one hundred and twenty-seven students with class
  13|                       sizes ranging from 25-26. Second grade has one hundred and twenty-three students with class
  14|                       sizes of 24-25 and the third grade has one hundred and thirty two students with class sizes of 26-
  15|                       27. We are utilizing all available space in the building to provide a child centered enriching
  16|                       educational program for all our students.
  17|                                     Our Professional Development this year focused on ALICE training, the Teacher
  18|                        Evaluation process, the Common Core curriculum, and Curriculum Mapping updates. The
  19|                        ALICE training is a proactive satety protocol used in the event of an active shooter. This is
  20|                       necessary to enhance our current safety procedures and processes. All of our classroom teachers
  21|                       continued to use Responsive Classroom strategies as part of our Academic/Social/Emotional
  22|                       Learning Curriculum which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules and logical
  23|                        consequences and academic choice.
  24|                                     The Primary School community continues to strive for academic excellence. Common
  25|                       assessments are in place in the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-3 in the areas
  26|                       of reading, writing, and math. These common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of
  27|                       teacher instruction and drives instructional programming for our students. We are now able to
  28|                       track progress throughout the year in a faster, more efficient manner in order to improve
  29|                       instruction. Students are identified quickly and additional resources and direct instruction are
  30|                       provided to address the areas of concern identified. The Primary School is a Title One school and
  31|                       we continue to utilize a Response to Intervention (Title One/RTI) approach to address student
  32|                       needs. Our tiered instructional system insures that all students have the instruction necessary to
  33|                       continue good progress with their learning. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area
  34|                       of Language Arts (Reading and Writing) and Math. The first tier involves the largest number of
  35|                       students, and is fitted for universal instruction in the classroom. Teachers are skilled in
  36|                        differentiating instruction to meet a large number of student needs in the classroom. The second
  37|                       tier combines more classroom teacher support and Title One Support for students. Title One/RTI
  38|                       services are based on student needs that are targeted, individual, and involve more intensive
  39|                       interventions. The third tier is the smallest and involves special services with very intense
  40|                       interventions. The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only individualize
  41|                       instruction to meet the needs of the students who are struggling with learning but those students
  42|                       who are doing well and need to strive to achieve higher levels of learning. With this model
  43|                       learning is more personalized and students are taught to become independent learners and
  44|                       problem solvers.
  45|                                                                                               99

===PAGE 100=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                         One hundred and four 3'd grade students participated in the spring 2014 MCAS
   2|            Assessment at the Primary School in March and May last year. The results were released and
   3|            sent home to parents on September 30th                         ". For our 3° graders this was their first experience taking
   4|            the state testing and the second time the MCAS testing has been completed at the Primary School.
   5|             We worked to make sure that students understand that they are capable and prepared to take this
   6|            assessment each year. Students prepared inspirational posters and we held our first MCAS Prep
   7|            Rally where the students sang the MCAS song. Teachers stress that the ongoing learning i an prepared inspiration
   8|            opportunities students have experienced over the last several years have prepared them to perform
   9|             well on this assessment.
  10|            In the area of English Language Arts there were 68 students or 65% who scored in the advanced
  11|            and proficient range of functioning (14 advanced, 54 proficient). 30 students or 29% were in the
  12|            needs improvement range (24 of which scored 230 or greater, 15 students were within three
  13|            points of achieving a proficient score). 6% (6) were in the warning range. These results are
  14|            similar to the previous year's result. This continues to be an area of focus for the Primary School.
  15|            In the area of Math 80 students or 78% of our 3 graders scored in the Advanced and Proficient
  16|            range of functioning (39 students- 38%-in the advanced range of functioning and 41 students
  17|            (40%) in the proficient range of functioning). 16% scored in the needs improvement range of
  18|            functioning with 10 students scoring 230 or greater in the High -needs improvement range. Six
  19|            students or 6% were in the warning range on the Math Assessment. Last year 85% of the
  20|            students who took the Math MCAS scored in the Advanced/Proficient range of functioning.
  21|            These results again are pretty similar to last years. We continue to maintain a higher number of
  22|            students in the advanced range of functioning compared to two years ago.
  23|            Our high needs students (students with disabilities, student requiring Title I services, students
  24|            requiring ELL instruction and low income students) continue to lag behind their peers both in
  25|            ELA and Math. Using the old No Child Left Behind comparison, Composite Performance Index
  26|            (CPI), which is used to measure the extent to which students are progressing toward proficiency
  27|            (a CPI of 100 is the target) our non-high need students, achieved a Composite Performance Index
  28|            (CPI) score of 94.1; our high need students received a CPI of 73.6 in ELA. In the area of math
  29|            our students' considered to have non-high needs (CPI) scored 97 and our high needs students
  30|            scored 77.1. We continue to address these student achievement gaps by using Tiered
  31|            Instructional strategies and direct instruction, Individualized Educational Plans and Curriculum
  32|            Accommodation plans.
  33|            Student Growth Data is not available for students in the 3'd grade as this is the first time they have
  34|            taken the MCAS assessment.
  35|            If you would like to access graphs and additional data related to MCAS please go to the following
  36|             web page:
  37|            http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/achievement_level.aspx?linkid=32&orgcode=01620010&orgty
  38|            pecode=6&
  39|                          Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and
  40|            supportive of each of our members. We have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and
  41|            recognize the importance of building a community where members feel a sense of belonging and
  42|            responsibility to contribute. Our students have a right to be in an environment which is safe,
  43|            promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.
  44|                          The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school issues, review the
  45|            annual budget for the school and to develop the School Improvement Plan as well as make
  46|            revisions to the school's handbook.

  40|                                                      TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL 2014-2015

===PAGE 104=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|            We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each
   2|           individual student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule allow
   3|            teachers time to work with students at their own individual skill level. The philosophy is to challenge
   4|            students who are high academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified
   5|            weaknesses. Teachers are able to work with students in a small group to answer individual questions or
   6|            provide more individualized support. We design this support model to allow students to feel more
   7|            confident, thus, perform better in the classroom. We will continue to refine our intervention block times
   8|            to best meet the needs of our students.
   9|            Turkey Hill Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes
  10|            provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health
  11|            and guidance. Our Second step developmental guidance program is now consistent in grades four through
  12|            seven and units focus on healthy decision making, empathy and communication as well as bullying
  13|            prevention to meet the intricate social needs of our middle school students.
  14|            Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, library media, and music classes that allows
  15|            them to explore their strengths and talents. This year, fourth and fifth grade students have been
  16|            participating in a word processing enrichment class to improve their keyboarding skills. Students also
  17|            have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and chorus during this block. This enrichment
  18|            block is in addition to the traditional special area classes students receive, and this block offers students
  19|            additional physical movement throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and
  20|            more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  21|            We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs developed
  22|            to promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. This year we have
  23|            refined and refreshed our utilization of responsive classroom and Developmental Design programs to
  24|            deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students meet with teachers in small groups to teach social
  25|            responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and promoting positive self-esteem.
  26|            Our seventh grade students are participating in themes such as embracing and respecting diversity and
  27|            grades four and five students were experiencing themes developed through our second step guidance
  28|            curriculum. We also incorporated an academic choice time for students in grades four and five. This is a
  29|            time developed for students to explore different interests or experiences or utilize time to complete
  30|            assignments that required additional time.
  31|            We also utilize best practices such as peer mediation and the Big Sibs program to foster and develop
  32|            positive peer leadership skills and effective peer mentoring. All of the social emotional programs have
  33|            been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals,
  34|            and promote community outreach for the younger generation.
  35|            Turkey Hill Middle school continues to provide educational services and support that ensures student
  36|            success and promotes lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to
  37|            our success is our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and
  38|            community who support our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through
  39|            their commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  40|            Respectfully submitted,
  41|            Timothy Santry
  42|            Principal

===PAGE 106=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                  LONENBURG HIGH SCHOOL
   2|               Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2014-2015. As time
   3|                has gone on, there is much more of a comfort level and stability in our grade 8-12 community.
   4|               This year, we oriented 128 eighth grade students, three teachers, and four staff members to our
   5|               building and school culture. With the assistance of 50 student leaders, we successfully
   6|               transitioned the eighth grade students into the LHS culture by having an eighth orientation on the
   7|               first day of school. We opened the year, as we do every year, with a guest speaker from the
   8|               Worcester County District Attorney's Office to discuss responsible Internet use, Facebook, cyber-
   9|               bullying and Internet safety with our incoming students and parents.
  10|               NEASC ACCREDITATION
  11|               Lunenburg High School is in the process of completing a Five Year Report for the Commission
  12|               on Public Secondary Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges. In 2010,
  13|               the Commission recommended that the school receive continued accreditation in the Association.
  14|               The Commission's decision was based upon review of an evaluation report prepared by a visiting
  15|               committee in April 2010. The Two Year Progress Report, submitted in January, 2013 received
  16|               high marks from the Commission reviewing our submission.
  17|               While the Commission lauded LHS in many areas, the Commission warned LHS in three areas
  18|               and asked for a Special Progress Report to be completed. Of major concern were issues with the
  19|               facility and meeting 21s century learning expectations. The Commission stated that the physical
  20|               plant significantly limits the school's ability to meet the educational needs of students. There is an
  21|               expectation to develop both a short- and long-term plan to fund and address the significant
  22|               facilities concerns including infrastructure and wiring issues, roof and window issues, limited
  23|               handicap accessibility and inadequate space.                                      In regards to curriculum, the commission
  24|               recommended the further development of a standard template to clearly articulate school-wide
  25|               expectations for student learning
  26|               As part of the Special Progress Report, the leadership team was charged with addressing the
  27|               recommendations                that were made by the Commission.                                            There are four highlighted
  28|               recommendations in the Commission's report and three additional recommendations identified
  29|               through a Special Progress Report. One of the "completed" recommendations was due to the
  30|               fact that on January 7, 2014 Lunenburg voters supported the design of a grade 6-12, middle/high
  31|               school building project, at a Special Town Meeting. On January 11, 2014, the town of Lunenburg
  32|               voted to approve a ballot proposal to build a new middle/high school. Voters cast 1,414 votes in
  33|               favor of the project and only 543 votes were in opposition to the project, in the special election.
  34|               The total cost of the project is $72,975,321. The leadership team reworked the school-wide
  35|               learning expectations so that they are aligned with the Common Core State Standards. The report
  36|               was submitted to the Commission on February 4, 2014. Lunenburg High School is currently
  37|               working on Core Values and Beliefs about student learning, which will be adopted by LHS once
  38|                the process is complete.
  39|               SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  40|               Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising
  41|               and collection endeavors. In the fall, three staff members were involved in a fundraiser to raise
  42|               money for the Lunenburg Food Pantry. Money was collected during lunch and the staff member
  43|               who raised the most money, kissed a pig. Jim Lattanzi, from Hollis Hills Farm, brought in the
  44|               pig for the Thanksgiving rally. Fun was had by all, and all of the proceeds were donated for the
  45|               holiday season.

   1|                              Having only been recently named the Superintendent-Director of Montachusett Regional Vocational
   2|                              Technical School, I am bonored and delighted to present the district's 2013-2014 annual report. It was a year
   3|                              marked by student achievement, faculty and staff distinctions, and leadership development - as well as

   1|                                                                                        Our Mission
   2|                              Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills,
   3|                              knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   4|                                                                                        Our District
   5|                              Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four-year career and technical high school serving the member towns of:
   6|                                        Ashburnham                                      Harvard                                       Princeton
   7|                                        Ashby                                          Holden                                          Royalston
   8|                                        Athol                                          Hubbardston                                     Sterling
   9|                                         Barre                                          Lumenburg                                      Templeton
  10|                                        Fitchburg                                      Petersham                                      Westminster
  11|                                        Gardner                                        Phillipston                                     Winchendon
  12|                                                                                         Leadership
  13|                              The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  14|                               administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise
  15|                              contribute to the success of the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the
  16|                              Superintendent and Principal, the team has been able to transform the school into one of the most sought-
  17|                              after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  18|                              Sheila M. Harrip, Superintendent-Director                             kristina Fazeau, Director of Academic Program
  19|                              Tom Browne, Principal                                                im Hachey, Director of Vocational Program

  23|                                                                                        Enrollment
  24|                             On June 1, 2014, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,403 students in grades nine through twelve.
  25|                            Students are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (54), Ashby (37), Athol (93),
  26|                            Baire (35), Fitchburg (364), Gardner (158), Harvard (3), Holden (68), Hubbardston (61), Lunenburg (70),
  27|                            Petersham (4), Phillipston (21), Princeton (17), Royalston (22), Sterling (56), Templeton (100), Westminster
  28|                             (T7), and Winchendon (133). The remaining thirty students were from out-of-district towns, including Clinton,
  29|                            Leominster, Natick, Orange, Pepperell, Rutland, Shirley, West Boylston, and Worcester.
  30|                             Throughout 2013-2014, Mouty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community
  31|                             members to leam about and visit the school In October 2013, approximately 1,000 district eighth graders
  32|                            participated in the annual "Tour Day" event Students toured our twenty-vocational/technical areas and
  33|                            learned about the school's challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracuricular
  34|                            programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in the evening with
  35|                            their family members, to further explore the facilities and talk with staff members.
  36|                            Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for
  37|                             admussions. 2013-2014 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for her, as the school recerved 730 applications for admission. Of those, 642 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class
  38|                             The balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because
  39|                                                                                               114

===PAGE 115=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                           there are only a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 367 freshmen and 19
   2|                           upperclassmen were admitted.
   3|                           The Vocational Interest Program (VIP.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit
   4|                           Monty Tech after school, and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of
   5|                           vocational/technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2013-2014
   6|                           school year, serving approximately 600 area students.
   7|                                                                                 Class of 2014 Awards
   8|                           Members of the Class of 2014 were awarded approximately $50,000 in scholarships. The Monty Tech
   9|                           Foundation generously provided $34,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $250 to
  10|                           $2,500. The Foundation also awarded $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state
  11|                           organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
  12|                           graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates
  13|                           themselves, are grateful for this support.
  14|                           articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country. Well over 60% of the graduating
  15|                           class of 2014 reported plans to enroll at either a 2-year college, 4-year college/university, or a technical/trade
  16|                           school upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will benefit by
  17|                           saving both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  18|                                                                                    Financial Report
  19|                           In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2013-2014, a great deal of effort was put forth
  20|                           by the School Committee, administration and staff. The final fiscal year 2013-2014 Educational Plan totaled
  21|                           $24,785,280, which represents a 2.0% increase over the 2012-2013 Educational Plan As a result of changes
  22|                          to the final FY14 State budget and the District's decision not to increase the already-approved community
  23|                           assessments, the District's FY14 budget was approximately $200,000 below the minimum spending required
  24|                           by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70.
  25|                           The District was audited in August 2014 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting fum of
  26|                           Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and a very good report is anticipated.
  27|                                                                                 Grants and Contracts
  28|                           Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds assist in providing many
  29|                           educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2014, state and federal grant sources
  30|                           provided the school with S842,780. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services,
  31|                           Social Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program
  32|                           Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Summer Academic Support.
  33|                           In addition to state and federal grant sources, Monty Tech was also the recipient of private and competitive
  34|                           grant awards totaling $525,000. Included in that total are a number of highly competitive awards, including a
  35|                           $400,000 award from the Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education, used to provide
  36|                                                                                              115

  42|                          Minolta High Speed Copier, a Konica Minolta Office Color Copier, and ten (10) new iMac computers, each

   3|                                                                                    Special Services
   4|                            During the 2013-2014 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided
   5|                             pecial services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two hundred students on Individual Education Plans (TEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individualized Sectior
   6|                            504 plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special education, the
   7|                            department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
   8|                            The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated health
   9|                               reening exams, and provides, when necessary, bealth intormation to the special education team for udent's IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full-time school social worker who participates i
  10|                            departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness,
  11|                            maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is also fortunate to have on staff a full-time
  12|                            psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three-
  13|                            year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech pathologist, who is available to assist students with
  14|                            disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Chur students also have access to the services of a
  15|                            full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for
  16|                            scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency treatment, as well as crisis intervention.
  17|                            The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the district's Special Education Program, which is
  18|                            reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and
  19|                            evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are
  20|                            used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational
  21|                            Technical School.

  32|                                                                             Practical Nursing Program
  33|                            The Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a caring
  34|                            manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in bealth status in diverse health care settings.
  35|                            This mission, which is consistent with the philosophy and goals of the Montachusett Regional Vocational
  36|                            Technical School District, accomplished the following:
  37|                                  • Identfies a strong relationship between academic and vocational preparation
  38|                                  • Stresses the importance of developing critical thinking skills to function safely, effectively, and
  39|                                     productively in an ever-changing technical and diverse society
  40|                                 • Supports the maintenance of a positive and caring learning and practice environment
  41|                                                                                              126

   1|                            In June 2014, thirty-three (33) students graduated and entered the mursing profession. The class achieved an
   2|                            initial NCLEX pass rate of 88%, and just over 91% of the 2014 graduates are currently working in the North
   3|                            Central area as LPNs in various health care settings from long term care, sub-acute care, clinics and prison
   4|                            health care.
   5|                            The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to develop the "LPN to BSN Bridge" relationship with
   6|                            Fitchburg State University. Additionally, the program is proud to report an expanded partnership with
   7|                            Fitchburg State University, as both institutions were recently awarded a grant known as the "Nurse of the
   8|                            Future" initiative, awarded by the state Department of Higher Education The grant funding will assist
   9|                            veterans hoping to transition from military to civilian healthcare fields, granting academic credits for previous
  10|                            military training and experience in related fields. The goal of this unique partnership is to have five students
  11|                            enrolled in the program at both Monty Tech and Fitchburg State University annually.

  20|                            area colleges, Monty Tech hopes to bring additional vocational-technical training opportunities to students that

  22|                            high school will not only save the students time and money, it validates they very rigorous educational
  23|                            programs in place here at Monty Tech. In 2014-2015, school officials hope to work closely with area college

  19|                            and culminate in an exciting field trip! Additionally, thanks to the continued generosity of the Monty Tech
  20|                            Foundation, scholarships will be available to students who qualify for free/reduced lunches, and all costs
  21|                            associated with the field trip will be covered for every participant.
